Well, I guess what made me finally come in is… I keep having these strange moments where things just don’t feel real. Same thing at home sometimes — my own living room feels unfamiliar, almost fake. Mostly I notice it at home and at my office. Those are the places where it’s happened enough that it scared me into coming in, honestly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really think it’s the unfamiliar places, no. I mean, if I’m somewhere new, I’m usually a little distracted anyway — looking for parking, figuring out where the restroom is, all that. My wife says I overthink directions, which is probably true.

But what brings me in is stranger than that. It’s my own house or my office, places I know inside and out, and suddenly they feel unreal. Like I’m there, but not really there… almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being in my own body. That’s what scares me, because those places shouldn’t feel unfamiliar at all.

Like I’ll be in my own house, or sitting at my desk at work — I’m a tax preparer, so I’m used to staring at forms and screens all day, you know — and suddenly it feels like I’m not really there. Like I’m watching myself from across the room.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding odd. The room looks the same, but it feels… distant, unreal. And I start thinking, “Why does my own office feel unfamiliar?” That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body, or that you're detached from yourself, like you're not really in control of your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much it. I don’t know that I’d say I’m completely not in control, exactly, but there are these moments where it feels like I’m sort of… outside of myself.\n\nLike I’ll be at my desk, looking at a client’s tax forms, and suddenly it’s like I’m watching myself sit there from across the room. Same thing at home sometimes — my own living room feels unfamiliar, almost fake. Not just a one-time thing.\n\nIt’ll come over me in my own house or at the office, and for a bit everything feels kind of unreal, like I’m seeing myself from across the room instead of just… being there. Those are the places where it’s happened enough that it scared me into coming in, honestly.\n\nI mean, my office is so ordinary — same desk, same stacks of tax paperwork, same little calculator I’ve had for years, though my wife keeps saying I should replace it because one of the buttons sticks — and then all of a sudden it feels like I’m not really sitting there. Sometimes they just seem to come over me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these feelings happening in response to specific events or thoughts, even outside of work or home, like during social situations or when you're alone thinking about something stressful?\n\nPatient: I’ve been trying to pin that down, honestly. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about work or some stressful thing, and maybe that sets it off, but the thing that really made me come in is that it keeps happening in places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there, same room I’ve sat in a thousand times, and suddenly it feels unreal. Almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed it as much in social situations. I’m usually more focused on, you know, making small talk or remembering someone’s name — I’m terrible with names, always have been. But alone, or just doing ordinary things at home or work, that’s when it stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced these feelings of detachment or unreality while in unfamiliar places, or during situations that are new or different for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think it’s the unfamiliar places, no. I mean, if I’m somewhere new, I’m usually a little distracted anyway — looking for parking, figuring out where the restroom is, all that. My wife says I overthink directions, which is probably true.\n\nBut what brings me in is stranger than that. It’s my own house or my office, places I know inside and out, and suddenly they feel unreal. Like I’m there, but not really there… almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being in my own body. That’s what scares me, because those places shouldn’t feel unfamiliar at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you weren’t real when you were in a completely unfamiliar place, like a new city or a strange room?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Maybe briefly, but I don’t think that’s really where I notice it. If I’m somewhere unfamiliar, I’m usually busy figuring things out — exits, parking, where I’m supposed to go. I know you’re asking about unfamiliar places, and maybe once in a while I feel a little off somewhere new, but that’s not really what stands out to me.

Like I’m seeing myself from across the room.\n\nAt home it’s similar. My own house feels unreal for a bit, like I’m there but not there. I mean… in a completely unfamiliar place, maybe I’d feel a little turned around, like anyone would. I remember going into one of those big office complexes where every hallway looks the same, and I was more annoyed than anything because I couldn’t find the suite number.

I’ll be sitting there and suddenly it feels like the room isn’t quite real, or like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. In a completely unfamiliar place? I don’t think that’s really where it happens. I mean, I can get thrown off if I’m somewhere new — like those medical buildings where the parking garage connects to the wrong floor, and then you’re wandering around looking for an elevator that makes sense — but that feels more like being confused or irritated.

Can you tell me what’s been bringing you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what made me finally come in is… I keep having these strange moments where things just don’t feel real. Like I’ll be in my own house, or sitting at my desk at work — I’m a tax preparer, so I’m used to staring at forms and screens all day, you know — and suddenly it feels like I’m not really there. Like I’m watching myself from across the room.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding odd. The room looks the same, but it feels… distant, unreal. And I start thinking, “Why does my own office feel unfamiliar?” That’s what’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're watching yourself from outside your body, or that you're detached from yourself, like you're not really in control of your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much it. I don’t know that I’d say I’m completely not in control, exactly, but there are these moments where it feels like I’m sort of… outside of myself.\n\nLike I’ll be at my desk, looking at a client’s tax forms, and suddenly it’s like I’m watching myself sit there from across the room. Same thing at home sometimes — my own living room feels unfamiliar, almost fake. It’s strange because nothing has changed, but it doesn’t feel like I’m really there in it.\n\nDoctor: Do these experiences happen only once in a while, or do they happen repeatedly and last for a while when they do?\n\nPatient: It happens repeatedly, yeah. Not just a one-time thing.\n\nIt’ll come over me in my own house or at the office, and for a bit everything feels kind of unreal, like I’m seeing myself from across the room instead of just… being there. Sometimes I’ll try to keep working because, you know, tax stuff doesn’t exactly wait for you to feel normal again — clients still want their forms done — but it can linger long enough that I really notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of being detached from yourself or your surroundings ever happen when you're not at work or at home, like in other places or situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it at home and at my office. Those are the places where it’s happened enough that it scared me into coming in, honestly.\n\nI mean, my office is so ordinary — same desk, same stacks of tax paperwork, same little calculator I’ve had for years, though my wife keeps saying I should replace it because one of the buttons sticks — and then all of a sudden it feels like I’m not really sitting there. Like I’m seeing myself from across the room.\n\nAt home it’s similar. My own house feels unreal for a bit, like I’m there but not there. I can’t really say I’ve noticed it much in other places, or maybe I just haven’t paid attention the same way. It’s mainly the house and office that stand out.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of detachment usually happen when you're feeling stressed or anxious, or do they come on suddenly without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It can be when I’m stressed, I think, but I don’t always see it coming. That’s part of what bothers me.\n\nI’ll just be in my office, surrounded by the usual papers and folders, and then suddenly the room feels… not real, somehow. Or I’m at home, and it’s my own house, but it feels strange, like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there.\n\nTaxes are stressful anyway — people bring in things at the last minute, and half the time they don’t know where their own forms are — but these moments don’t always line up neatly with that. Sometimes they just seem to come over me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these feelings happening in response to specific events or thoughts, even outside of work or home, like during social situations or when you're alone thinking about something stressful?\n\nPatient: I’ve been trying to pin that down, honestly. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about work or some stressful thing, and maybe that sets it off, but the thing that really made me come in is that it keeps happening in places that should feel completely normal — my own house, my office.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there, same room I’ve sat in a thousand times, and suddenly it feels unreal. Almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic.\n\nI don’t know that I’ve noticed it as much in social situations. I’m usually more focused on, you know, making small talk or remembering someone’s name — I’m terrible with names, always have been. But alone, or just doing ordinary things at home or work, that’s when it stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced these feelings of detachment or unreality while in unfamiliar places, or during situations that are new or different for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think it’s the unfamiliar places, no. I mean, if I’m somewhere new, I’m usually a little distracted anyway — looking for parking, figuring out where the restroom is, all that. My wife says I overthink directions, which is probably true.\n\nBut what brings me in is stranger than that. It’s my own house or my office, places I know inside and out, and suddenly they feel unreal. Like I’m there, but not really there… almost like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being in my own body. That’s what scares me, because those places shouldn’t feel unfamiliar at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt detached from yourself or that things around you weren’t real when you were in a completely unfamiliar place, like a new city or a strange room?\n\nPatient: Not so much in a new city or a strange room, no. I mean, I might feel thrown off if I don’t know where I am — I get turned around easily, and then I’m irritated at myself — but that’s different.\n\nWhat’s been bothering me is when it happens somewhere I know. My own house, or my office. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly it’s like the room isn’t quite real, or like I’m watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. That’s the part that feels so unsettling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced that same sense of things feeling unreal or like you're watching yourself from outside your body in unfamiliar places, even if just briefly?\n\nPatient: Maybe briefly, but I don’t think that’s really where I notice it. If I’m somewhere unfamiliar, I’m usually busy figuring things out — exits, parking, where I’m supposed to go. I was in a courthouse once for a client meeting and spent ten minutes looking for the right office, which is embarrassing for someone who works with paperwork all day.\n\nBut the thing that really bothers me is when it happens at home or in my office. That’s the part that feels wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced that same sense of things feeling unreal or like you're watching yourself from outside your body in unfamiliar places, even if just for a short moment?\n\nPatient: Right, yes… I know I keep circling back to this, sorry. It’s my own house or my office, places I know inside and out, and suddenly they feel unreal.
